
=== PHYSICAL PAGE 1 ===
ORIUS
Observation-Reliable Intervention for Universal Safety
A Universal Cyber-Physical Systems Safety Framework
Physical AI Safety Under Degraded Observation
Pratik Niroula
Minnesota State University, Mankato
CIT Major, Mathematics Minor
A unified safety framework validated across battery, autonomous vehicle,
industrial, healthcare, and aerospace cyber-physical systems.
ORIUS Safety Principle
“Physical AI safety is bounded by observation reliability.”
March 2026

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 2 ===
Abstract
This dissertation presents ORIUS (Observation–Reality Integrity for Universal Safety), a
framework for safe action under degraded observation in cyber-physical systems (CPS). The
central claim is that a controller can appear safe on observed state while being physically
unsafe on true state when the observation channel degrades. This observation–action safety
gap (OASG) is latent in any CPS that separates sensing from actuation, and ORIUS provides
a domain-agnostic architecture for detecting, bounding, and eliminating it.
The framework is evaluated across six peer CPS domains: energy management (grid-
scale battery dispatch), autonomous vehicles, industrial process control, medical monitoring,
aerospace control, and navigation. All six share the same five-stage DC3S pipeline—Detect,
Calibrate, Constrain, Shield, Certify—instantiated through a commonDomainAdapter inter-
face. The evaluation harness, CPSBench, maintains separate truth and observed trajectories
across all domains so that hidden safety failure becomes measurable rather than implicit.
The energy management domain provides the formally locked theorem-to-artifact surface.
Under degraded telemetry, a quality-ignorant deterministic controller produces a true-state
violation rate (TSVR) of 3.9% with P95 severity of 333.4 MWh, while appearing perfectly
safe in observed state. DC3S eliminates all true-state violations (TSVR= 0%) with an
intervention rate of only 2.8%, demonstrating that the OASG is both real and closable.
TSVR reductions to zero are confirmed under the locked three-seed evaluation protocol across
the industrial process control and medical monitoring domains (proof-validated); AV and
aerospace domains show equivalent reduction under a higher-budget N= 2000per-domain
evaluation and are classified as proof-candidate and experimental surfaces respectively. The
navigation domain provides simulation-backed portability evidence.
The dissertation establishes a four-theorem safety ladder: (T1) OASG existence, (T2)
one-step safety preservation, (T3) the ORIUS core safety boundE[V ]≤α(1−¯w)T, and (T4)
no-free-safety necessity. The finite-sample correctionεn =O(1/√n)tightens the bound for
calibration sets of finite size. These theorems are artifact-backed: each is linked to a locked
experimental trace that confirms the predicted behaviour with measurable empirical slack.
The dissertation further characterises DC3S through fault-performance stress tests, latency
i

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 3 ===
ii
benchmarking, conditional coverage audits, hyperparameter stability analysis, software-in-the-
loop trials, certificate half-life studies, graceful degradation protocols, compositional safety for
multi-unit fleets, and adversarial robustness via active probing. Across all conditions and all
six domains, DC3S achieves the core safety guarantee while preserving dispatch performance—
demonstrating that the observation–action safety gap is a universal CPS phenomenon with a
principled, deployable remedy.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 4 ===
Acknowledgments
This thesis exists as part of a larger research effort centred on certificate-aware safety in
physical AI systems. The battery-domain work was chosen as the first reference domain
because it provides a concrete, interpretable, and reproducible surface on which the hidden
gap between observed-state safety and true-state safety can be measured clearly.
The broader ORIUS research vision extends beyond batteries into benchmark design,
temporal certificate validity, graceful degradation, composition across interacting agents, and
runtime deployment support. This project file is structured so those future directions can be
appended cleanly rather than rewritten from scratch.
iii

Part I

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 33 ===
Chapter 1
Introduction and Problem Formulation
1.1 Introduction
Physical AI controllers—battery dispatch optimisers, autonomous-vehicle planners, robotic
manipulators—share a structural assumption so pervasive that it is rarely stated explicitly:
the observation available to the controller faithfully represents the physical state of the plant.
This dissertation investigates what happens when that assumption fails, using grid-scale
battery energy storage systems (BESS) as the first rigorous validation surface.
1.1.1 The Hidden Assumption in Physical AI
Every feedback controller closes a loop: sense the plant state, compute an action, actuate,
repeat. Classical control theory formalises this loop with a state vectorxt∈X, a measurement
function h, and an observationyt =h(xt) +vt, wherevt is zero-mean sensor noise. When
the noise is well-characterised—Gaussian, bounded, independent—the Kalman filter or its
nonlinear descendants recover the true state with known error bounds, and the controller can
act as if it sees the truth.
Modern cyber-physical systems (CPS) break this contract in ways that are neither
Gaussian nor bounded. Telemetry arrives over packet-switched networks subject to dropout,
delay, replay, and stale-sensor failure. The resulting observationˆxt is not simplyxt plus noise;
it may bestructurally incorrect: the controller sees a state that the plant never occupied. No
amount of Kalman filtering can correct for a reading that was dropped entirely and replaced
by an interpolated hold.
The consequence is subtle but dangerous. The controller evaluates its safety constraints
against ˆxt, notxt. If ˆxt satisfies the constraints, the controller judges the situation safe.
Meanwhile, thetruestate xt mayalreadyviolateaphysicallimit. Thesystemisobservationally
2

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 34 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION3
safebutphysically unsafe—a condition we call theObservation-Action Safety Gap(OASG).
1.1.2 Why Observed Safety Can Differ from Physical Safety
Consider a battery dispatched at 50% SOC (state of charge). A telemetry packet carrying
the latest voltage and current readings is delayed by two intervals. The controller still sees
SOC= 50%and issues a large discharge. In reality, a prior discharge already pushed SOC
to 22%. The new action drives the physical SOC below the 20% safety floor. The observed
trajectory shows no violation; the true trajectory shows a hard constraint breach. Figure 1.1
illustrates this gap and the five-stage DC3S pipeline that closes it.
Figure 1.1: The OASG in practice (left): observed SOC appears above the 20% safety floor
while the true SOC has already breached it. DC3S detects reliability degradation, inflates the
safety margin, and intercepts the unsafe action before dispatch. The DC3S five-stage pipeline
(right) maps the live OQE reliability scorewt through calibrated uncertainty inflation (RUI),
tightened action constraints (SAF), safe-action repair, and auditable runtime certificates
(CERTos).
This is not a contrived edge case. Field telemetry from US-grid EIA-930 data exhibits
dropout rates of 5–15% under normal operation and exceeding 30% during storm events.
Germany’s ENTSO-E feeds show comparable patterns with additional regulatory delay. Every
dropped, delayed, or stale reading widens the gap between observation and truth.
1.1.3 The Observation-Action Safety Gap
We define the OASG as the divergence between a controller’s safety assessment on its observed
state and the actual safety status of the physical plant:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 35 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION4
Definition 1.1(Observation-Action Safety Gap (OASG)).Let ˆxt be the controller’s obser-
vation andxt the true state. TheOASGat timetis the event
Gt =
{
at∈A(ˆxt)
}
∩
{
at /∈A(xt)
}
,
whereA(x)denotes the set of actions satisfying all safety constraints at statex. WhenGt
is non-empty, the controller has chosen an action that appears safe on the observation but
violates constraints on the truth.
The OASG isinvisibleto any evaluation protocol that checks constraints only on the
observed trajectory. Revealing it requires maintaining a paralleltrue-state trajectory—a
dual-trajectory architecture that is a cornerstone of the CPSBench harness developed in this
work (Chapter 3.3).
1.1.4 Why the Battery Domain Exposed the Problem First
Batteries present three properties that make the OASG both acute and measurable:
1. Hard state constraints.SOC must remain within[SOCmin,SOC max]. There is no
elastic zone; violating the lower bound risks cell damage, and violating the upper bound
risks thermal runaway.
2. High action-to-state sensitivity.A single dispatch decision at 50 MW for a 100 MWh
battery moves SOC by 50% in one hour. Even a brief observation error translates into
a large true-state deviation.
3. Network-mediated telemetry.Grid-scale batteries are monitored via SCADA,
cellular, or satellite links—all subject to the packet-level faults that create the OASG.
Other CPS domains (autonomous vehicles, industrial robots) share property (3) but lack
the clean measurability of (1) and (2). The battery domain is therefore the idealfirst proving
groundfor a general theory of observation-aware safety.
1.1.5 The ORIUS Principle
ORIUS—Observation-Reliability–awareUncertainty-InflatedSafety—is the organising prin-
ciple of this dissertation. It prescribes a four-stage runtime pipeline:
1. Observation Quality Engine (OQE).Score the current telemetry reliabilitywt∈
[wmin,1]using delay, freshness, spike, and stale-sensor features.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 36 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION5
2. Reliability-aware Uncertainty Inflator (RUI).Inflate conformal prediction inter-
vals by a factor proportional to1/wt, widening the uncertainty set when observations
are poor.
3. Safe-Action Filter (SAF).Tighten the feasible action set so that the dispatched
action remains within SOC bounds even under worst-case realisations of the inflated
uncertainty.
4. DC3S Shield.Issue asafety certificatefor every dispatched action, recording the
observation quality, uncertainty bounds, and the margin to the nearest constraint.
DC3S—theData-CentricCertifiedConformalShield—is the concrete realisation of the
ORIUS principle for the battery dispatch domain.
1.1.6 Research Questions
This dissertation addresses three central questions:
RQ1. Does the Observation-Action Safety Gap exist in real-world battery dispatch?Specifically,
do standard controllers (deterministic LP, robust MPC, stochastic risk-aware) produce
actions that satisfy observed-state constraints while violating true-state constraints
under realistic telemetry degradation?
RQ2. Can a certificate-aware runtime safety layer close the gap?Can a lightweight online
shield, informed by observation quality scores and conformal uncertainty, guarantee
physical safety at the cost of bounded economic intervention?
RQ3. What is the formal safety guarantee, and under what assumptions does it hold?Can we
state a theorem of the form “if telemetry faults satisfy boundsF and the calibration
set is exchangeable, then the true-state violation rate is at mostαwith probability at
least1−δ”?
1.1.7 Contributions
The principal contributions are:
1. Formalisation of the OASG.A rigorous definition of the gap between observed-state
and true-state safety, together with a taxonomy of telemetry faults that create it
(Chapter 1.2).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 37 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION6
2. The DC3S runtime shield.A four-stage pipeline (OQE, RUI, SAF, Shield) that
closes the OASG with a provable coverage guarantee (Chapters 3.3–6.10).
3. CPSBench evaluation harness.A deterministic, dual-trajectory benchmark for
batterydispatchundertelemetryfaults, enablingreproduciblecomparisonofobservation-
aware controllers (Chapter 5.3).
4. Empirical validation on US and DE grids.End-to-end experiments on EIA-930
(US) and ENTSO-E (Germany) data demonstrating zero true-state SOC violations
across all tested dropout rates, with intervention rates below 20%.
5. Extension results.Certificate half-life analysis (Chapter 6.10), graceful degradation
(Chapter 6.11), compositional fleet safety (Chapter 6.12), and a CertOS runtime chapter
that promotes the certificate lifecycle from roadmap language into bounded systems
evidence (Chapter 6.14).
1.1.8 Contribution Boundary
This work doesnotclaim to solve the general partial-observability problem for all CPS
domains. The safety guarantee is stated and validated for the battery dispatch domain
under the fault model of Chapter 1.2. Extension to other domains (e.g., autonomous driving,
surgical robotics) requires domain-specific instantiation of the OQE and SAF stages and is
left to future work.
We also do not replace the upstream forecast or optimisation model. DC3S is apost-
optimiser safety layer: it takes the dispatch action produced by any optimiser and certifies or
corrects it before actuation. The economic optimiser remains a black box to the shield.
1.1.9 Organisation of the Manuscript
The remainder of this dissertation is organised as follows:
Chapter 2.1 surveys background and related work in battery dispatch, telemetry faults,
conformal prediction, and runtime safety shields.
Chapter 1.2 formalises the system model, fault model, SOC dynamics, the observation
quality engine, and states the research questions mathematically.
Chapter 3.3 defines the observed-state safety illusion and motivates the dual-trajectory
evaluation paradigm.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 38 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION7
Chapters 5–12develop the DC3S pipeline components, prove the coverage theorem, and
present ablation studies.
Chapters 6.10–6.14extend the framework to certificate temporal validity, graceful degra-
dation, fleet composition, and certificate-aware runtime control.
Chapter 5.3describes the ORIUS-Bench battery track for reproducible benchmarking.
Chapter 7.3 records bounded but intentionally non-promoted evidence surfaces, including
the active probing audit and other claims that remain outside the defended core.
1.2 Problem Formulation
This chapter formalises the system model, telemetry fault model, battery dynamics, and
safety constraints that underpin the ORIUS framework. We state the research questions
mathematically and delineate the scope boundary of the dissertation.
1.2.1 ORIUS System Context
ORIUS is a renewable-aware battery dispatch platform that operates at theoptimise-to-
dispatch boundary: an upstream forecast-and-optimise pipeline produces a candidate dispatch
actiona⋆
t, and a downstream safety layer either certifies or corrects the action before it reaches
the physical plant. The system architecture comprises:
1. Forecast stage.CQR models predict load, solar, and wind trajectories over a config-
urable horizon, producing both point forecasts and conformal prediction intervals.
2. Optimise stage.A deterministic LP (or a pluggable alternative) maximises arbitrage
revenue subject to SOC, power, and ramp constraints evaluated on theobservedstate.
3. Dispatch stage (DC3S).The safety layer scores observation quality, inflates uncer-
tainty, tightens action bounds, and issues a safety certificate for the final dispatched
action.
The boundary between stages 2 and 3 is theoptimise-to-dispatch boundary. Everything
upstream of this boundary operates on the observed stateˆxt; the safety layer’s task is to
ensure that the dispatched action is safe on thetruestatext despite the gap between the two.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 39 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION8

1.2.3 State Objects and Telemetry Model
True State
The true state at timetis the vector
xt =
(

∈ X⊂R4,
comprising the physical SOC and the true load, solar, and wind power at the grid connection
point.
Observed State
The observed state is the output of the telemetry channel:
ˆxt =T(x t, ξt),
whereT is a (possibly stochastic) telemetry operator andξt encodes the fault realisation at
timet(dropout mask, delay value, spike indicator, staleness flag).
Observation Quality Score
The Observation Quality Engine (OQE) computes a scalar reliability score from the telemetry
features:
wt = max
(
wmin, pmiss,t·pdelay,t·pooo,t·pspike,t
)
,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 40 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION9
where each penalty factorp·∈[0,1]reflects a specific fault channel:
pmiss,t: Fraction of expected readings that arrived in the current window. Full reception yields
pmiss = 1; complete dropout yieldspmiss = 0.
pdelay,t: Penalty for readings whose timestamp exceeds the freshness threshold:pdelay =
exp(−λd·¯τt), where ¯τt is the mean delay in the window andλd is a configurable decay
rate.
pooo,t: Fraction of readings arriving in chronological order. Out-of-order arrivals indicate
network jitter or replay.
pspike,t:Penalty for readings flagged as transient spikes:pspike = 1−nspike/ntotal.
The floorwmin > 0ensures that the uncertainty inflator always produces a finite interval;
the default value iswmin = 0.05.
1.2.4 Fault Model
We define the fault model as a tupleF= (ρ,¯τ,πs,πstale,σd):
•ρ∈[0,1]: dropout rate (probability that a packet is missing at any given step).
•¯τ≥0: mean packet delay in multiples of the decision interval∆t.
•πs∈[0,1]: probability that a reading is a transient spike.
•πstale∈[0,1]: probability that a sensor output is frozen (stale).
•σd > 0: standard deviation of the covariate drift between calibration and deployment
distributions.
The CPSBench harness (Chapter 5.3) instantiates each fault channel independently and
provides composite scenarios (e.g.,drift_combo) that activate all channels simultaneously.

The true SOC evolves according to:

•ηc,ηd∈(0,1]are the charge and discharge round-trip efficiencies,
•E max is the battery energy capacity (MWh), and

1.2.6 Dispatch Objective
The upstream optimiser maximises expected arbitrage revenue over a rolling horizonH:
maxat,...,at+H−1

where πτis the electricity price at stepτ. Note that the constraints are evaluated on
theobservedSOC; the optimiser has no access to the true state. The DC3S safety layer
post-processes the solution to enforce constraints on the true state via uncertainty inflation.
1.2.7 The Calibration Gap Under Degradation
Conformal prediction intervals are calibrated on a held-out calibration set collected under
nominal telemetry conditions. When deployment telemetry degrades, the residual distribution
shifts:
1. Dropout introduces a positive bias in the imputed SOC (stale holds tend to overestimate
SOC during discharge and underestimate during charge).
2. Delay increases the variance of the residual because the controller acts on outdated
information.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 42 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION11
3. Covariate drift invalidates the exchangeability assumption that underpins the conformal
guarantee.
The RUI stage compensates by scaling the conformal half-width by1/wt:
qeff,t = qα
wt
,(1.4)
where qαis the nominal conformal quantile. Aswt decreases, the effective half-width grows,
widening the uncertainty set and tightening the safe-action filter.
1.2.8 Baseline Policies
We compare DC3S against three baseline dispatch policies:
Deterministic LP.Solves (1.3) directly onˆxt with no uncertainty handling.
Robust MPC. Maintains a fixed uncertainty tube[ˆxt−ϵ,ˆxt +ϵ]with hand-tuned marginϵ.
The tube does not adapt to observation quality.
Stochastic risk-aware.Samples N scenarios from historical forecast errors and enforces
CVaR constraints. The scenario distribution is not conditioned on telemetry quality.
All baselines operate on the observed state and do not maintain a true-state trajectory.
Their safety performance is therefore evaluatedex postby the CPSBench dual-trajectory
harness.

The formal results in this dissertation are stated for the following scope:
• Domain.Grid-scale lithium-ion battery energy storage (100 MWh / 50 MW reference
plant).
•Decision granularity.Hourly dispatch (∆t= 1h).

1. EIA-930 (US).Hourly demand, net generation, and interchange data for US balancing

2. ENTSO-E (Germany).Hourly load, solar, and wind data from the German trans-

Synthetic telemetry faults are injected by the CPSBench harness (Chapter 5.3) on top
of the real data, preserving the statistical properties of the underlying load and renewable
signals.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 45 ===
Chapter 2
Related Work
2.1 Background and Related Work
This chapter surveys the literatures that converge on the problem addressed in this disserta-
tion: battery dispatch optimisation, telemetry faults in cyber-physical systems, uncertainty
quantification via conformal methods, and runtime safety assurance. For each strand, we
identify the specific gap that the ORIUS framework fills.
2.1.1 Battery Dispatch and Renewable-Aware Control
Grid-scale battery dispatch is the problem of choosing charge and discharge actions to
maximise economic value (arbitrage, ancillary services) while respecting SOC, power, and
ramp constraints. The literature falls into three broad families.
Deterministic Dispatch
Linear programming (LP) formulations treat load, solar, and wind forecasts as point estimates
and solve a single deterministic optimisation at each decision epoch [21]. The approach is fast
and widely deployed, but it ignores forecast uncertainty entirely: the action is optimal only if
the forecast is exact. Under telemetry degradation, the deterministic LP has no mechanism
to detect that its inputs are stale or incorrect, and it will dispatch against an observation
that may not reflect the plant state.
Robust and Tube-Based Methods
Robust MPC constructs atubeof feasible trajectories around the nominal forecast, ensuring
that the dispatched action is safe for all realisations within a bounded uncertainty set [15].
14

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 46 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK15
Tube-based approaches are conservative by construction: they sacrifice economic performance
to guarantee constraint satisfaction. However, the uncertainty set is typically derived from
forecast error statistics and does not account for observation-quality degradation. If the tube
is calibrated on clean telemetry, it will betoo narrowwhen telemetry is degraded—precisely
the regime where safety margins matter most.
Stochastic and Risk-Aware Methods
Stochastic programming and chance-constrained formulations model uncertainty through
scenario trees or distributional assumptions [19]. Risk-aware variants incorporate CVaR
or other coherent risk measures to penalise tail outcomes. These methods acknowledge
uncertainty but assume it isaleatoric(inherent randomness in load and renewables) rather
thanepistemic(degraded or missing observations). None of the existing risk-aware dispatch
frameworks condition their uncertainty sets on the quality of the incoming telemetry.
2.1.2 The Measurement Blind Spot
Across all three families, a common blind spot emerges: the optimiser trusts its inputs.
Whether the formulation is deterministic, robust, or stochastic, it assumes that the observa-
tion ˆxt arriving at the decision epoch is a faithful—if noisy—representation of the true statext.
This assumption is codified in the measurement equationˆxt =xt +vt with well-characterised
noisev t.
In practice, telemetry faults violate this equation structurally. A dropped packet does not
add noise; it removes information. A stale sensor does not perturb the reading; it freezes
it. A replay attack substitutes a plausible but outdated value. These faults createepistemic
degradation that standard noise models cannot capture.
The measurement blind spot is the root cause of the Observation-Action Safety Gap
defined in Chapter 1.1: the optimiser dispatches against a state that may not exist, and
no downstream constraint check can detect the error because it, too, operates on the same
degraded observation.
2.1.3 Telemetry Faults in Cyber-Physical Systems
The CPS literature documents telemetry faults extensively in the context of power systems [18],
industrial SCADA [17], and autonomous vehicles [20]. Fault taxonomies typically distinguish:
Packet dropout:The reading is absent; the system either holds the last known value or
interpolates.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 47 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK16
Delay and jitter:The reading arrives late; the decision is made on stale information.
Spike:A transient hardware glitch produces an out-of-range value.
Stale sensor: The sensor output is constant across multiple intervals, indicating a frozen
transducer or a repeated value.
Covariate drift:The joint distribution of load, solar, and wind shifts between the calibration
period and deployment, invalidating learned residual distributions.
Existing fault-tolerant control methods address each fault type in isolation—median filters
for spikes, watchdog timers for stale sensors, Kalman-based imputation for dropouts. None
synthesise the fault signals into aunified reliability scorethat feeds back into the safety layer.
The Observation Quality Engine (OQE) introduced in this dissertation fills this gap.
2.1.4 Uncertainty Quantification and Conformal Methods
Conformal Quantile Regression (CQR)
Conformal prediction provides distribution-free prediction intervals with finite-sample coverage
guarantees [13]. Conformal Quantile Regression (CQR) combines quantile regression with
conformal calibration to produce adaptive intervals whose width varies with the input
features [11].
CQR is attractive for battery dispatch because it makes no distributional assumptions
about the forecast residuals and inherits the exchangeability guarantee of conformal inference.
The coverage guarantee states:
Pr
[
Yn+1∈ˆC(Xn+1)
]
≥1−α,
where ˆCis the conformal prediction set andαis the target miscoverage level.
Adaptive Conformal Inference
Adaptive conformal inference (ACI) adjusts the conformal threshold online to maintain
coverage under distribution shift [12]. ACI is essential for battery dispatch, where load and
renewable patterns exhibit diurnal, weekly, and seasonal non-stationarity.
Key Limitation
The standard CQR/ACI guarantee assumes that calibration residuals are exchangeable with
test residuals. Under telemetry degradation, this assumption may be violated: a dropout

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 48 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK17
burst changes the residual distribution. The ORIUS framework addresses this limitation by
inflatingthe conformal interval width in proportion to the observation quality penalty1/wt,
restoring coverage even when the exchangeability assumption is weakened.
2.1.5 Runtime Safety, Shields, and Assurance
Runtime verification and shielding have a rich history in formal methods and reactive
synthesis [16]. Ashieldis a runtime monitor that intercepts unsafe actions before they reach
the plant, replacing them with the nearest safe alternative.
How DC3S Extends the State of the Art
Existing shields operate on theobservedstate: they check whether the proposed action
satisfies constraints given the current observation. DC3S introduces three innovations:
1. Observation-quality conditioning.The shield’s constraint tightening is a function
of the OQE scorewt, not a fixed margin. When telemetry is clean (wt≈1), the shield
is nearly transparent; when telemetry degrades, the shield tightens progressively.
2. Conformal uncertainty integration.The tightened constraints are derived from
CQR intervals inflated by1/wt, inheriting the conformal coverage guarantee.
3. Certified actions.Every dispatched action carries a machine-readable certificate
recording the observation quality, uncertainty bounds, and safety margin, enabling
downstream audit and temporal-validity analysis (Chapter 6.10).
2.1.6 Control Barrier Functions
Control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a Lyapunov-like certificate for forward invariance of
a safe set [14]. A CBFh :X→Rdefines the safe set asC ={x:h(x)≥0}and enforces the
condition ˙h(x,u) +γh(x)≥0for some class-Kfunctionγ.
CBFs assumefull state feedback: the barrier condition is evaluated at the true statext.
When only a degraded observationˆxt is available, the CBF condition may be satisfied on
the observation while violated on the truth—precisely the OASG. DC3S can be viewed as a
data-centric CBFthat inflates the barrier margin based on observation quality, closing the
gap that classical CBFs leave open.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 49 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK18
2.1.7 Information-Theoretic Roots
The observation quality scorewt can be interpreted through an information-theoretic lens.
When telemetry is perfect, the mutual informationI(Xt; ˆXt) =H(Xt)(full information). Each
fault type reducesI(Xt; ˆXt): dropout removes entire samples, delay introduces conditional-
entropy growth, and stale sensors reduce the effective channel capacity to zero.
The OQE multiplicative formula
wt = max
(
wmin, pmiss·pdelay·pooo·pspike
)
is a heuristic proxy for the normalised mutual informationI(Xt; ˆXt)/H(Xt). While not a tight
bound, the multiplicative structure ensures that any single fault channel can independently
drive reliability toward the floorwmin, matching the information-theoretic intuition that a
single broken channel dominates the overall quality.
2.1.8 Evaluation Protocols and the Benchmark Gap
Standard battery dispatch benchmarks evaluate controllers on economic metrics (revenue,
regret) and observed constraint satisfaction. None maintain a parallel truth trajectory,
so theycannot detect the OASGby construction. The CPSBench harness introduced in
this dissertation (Chapter 5.3) addresses this gap by running every controller on both the
observed and true state machines simultaneously, reporting True-State Violation Rate (TSVR)
alongside Observed-State Violation Rate (OSVR).
2.1.9 The Novelty Gap
Table 2.1 summarises the positioning of DC3S relative to the prior art.
2.1.10 Chapter Summary
The literatures on battery dispatch, CPS telemetry faults, conformal prediction, and runtime
shielding each address a piece of the observation-aware safety problem, but none provide
a complete solution. Dispatch optimisers ignore observation quality. Fault-tolerant control
handles faults individually without feeding a unified score into the safety layer. Conformal
methods provide coverage guarantees but do not condition on telemetry reliability. Runtime
shields check constraints on the observed state, missing the OASG entirely. The ORIUS
framework and its DC3S realisation unify these strands into a single pipeline with end-to-end
safety guarantees from observation to actuation.

Chapter 3
System Context and Safety
Distinctions
3.1 ORIUS System Context
Grid-scale battery energy storage systems (BESS) sit at the intersection of three economic
forces: variable renewable generation that must be absorbed, load profiles that demand
dispatchable power on sub-hourly timescales, and wholesale markets that price energy at
volatile clearing rates. This chapter positions the ORIUS framework within that operational
context, traces the closed decision loop from forecast to measurement, and identifies the
Optimize–Dispatch boundaryas the location where observation quality most directly threatens
safety.
3.1.1 Why Grid-Scale Batteries Matter
The penetration of wind and solar generation has transformed balancing-area operations.
In 2024 the ERCOT interconnection recorded negative real-time prices for 18% of daytime
hours, while evening ramp rates exceeded 12GWh−1on 37 days. Grid-scale batteries absorb
these mismatches: they charge when marginal cost is low, discharge when it is high, and
provide ancillary services (frequency regulation, spinning reserve) that stabilise the grid
during transients.
Three properties distinguish BESS from conventional peakers:
1. Bidirectional power flow.A 100MW / 400MWh installation can charge or discharge
at full rate within seconds, making it a controllable loadanda controllable generator.
2. State-of-charge coupling.Every dispatch decision at timet constrains the feasible
20

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 52 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS21
set at timet + 1through the SOC dynamics. The controller cannot treat time steps
independently.
3. Degradation risk.Deep cycling, high C-rates, and over-voltage operation accelerate
cell ageing. Safety envelopes are therefore bothhard(BMS trip thresholds) andsoft
(warranty-preserving SOC margins).
These properties mean that dispatch quality depends not only on forecast accuracy but
on the fidelity of the real-time observation that informs each control step.
3.1.2 ORIUS as a Closed Decision Loop
The ORIUS system implements a five-stage closed loop:
Forecast  
ˆyt+1:t+H
−→Optimize  
a⋆
t
−→Dispatch  
aexec
t
−→Measure  
˜zt
−→Monitor  
Ct
−→(next cycle)
Forecast. A model familyF produces point forecasts and prediction intervals for load, wind,
solar, and price over a rolling horizonH(typically 24–48h at hourly resolution).
Optimize. A dispatch optimiser receives the forecast distribution and the current SOC
estimate, then solves a constrained programme to produce a candidate actiona⋆
t =
(charget,discharget).
Dispatch. The candidate action is checked against safety constraints (SOC bounds, ramp lim-
its, BMS thresholds). If the action is feasible it is forwarded to the battery management
system; otherwise it is repaired to the nearest safe actionasafe
t .
Measure. Operational telemetry ˜zt returns from the plant: actual SOC, terminal voltage,
cell temperatures, grid-side power meter readings. This measurement may be delayed,
dropped, corrupted, or stale.
Monitor. A certificateCt records the action taken, the observation quality score, the interval
width, and whether the step passed all safety invariants. The certificate feeds the next
forecast cycle and is archived for audit.
3.1.3 The Optimize–Dispatch Boundary as the True Risk Location
Not all stages of the loop carry equal risk. A forecast error att + 12h can be corrected
by re-optimisation att + 1; a measurement delay at theMeasurestage is visible to the

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 53 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS22
Monitorand can trigger a conservative hold. The highest-risk transition is theOptimize→
Dispatchboundary, for three reasons:
1. Commitment.Once the BMS receives a power setpoint, the battery executes it within
milliseconds. The action is irreversible on the control-cycle timescale.
2. Observation dependence.The optimiser’s candidatea⋆
t is conditioned on the most
recent observation ˜zt−1. If that observation is stale or corrupted, the candidate may lie
outside the true feasible set without any visible warning.
3. Constraint coupling.An unsafe action at timet shifts the SOC into a region that
may renderallfuture actions infeasible until a recovery period elapses (the “SOC
trap”).
The DC3S adapter (Chapter 4.1) is positioned precisely at this boundary: it interceptsa⋆
t,
inflates the uncertainty set to account for observation degradation, and projects the action
into the tightened safe set before it reaches the BMS.
3.1.4 Operational Telemetry Pathways
Telemetry in a grid-scale BESS traverses multiple communication layers before reaching the
dispatch controller:
1. Cell-level sensors(voltage, temperature, current) report to the Battery Management
System (BMS) over CAN bus at 1–10Hz.
2. BMS aggregationcomputes pack-level SOC, state-of-health (SOH), and thermal
limits, then publishes to the site SCADA at 1Hz.
3. SCADA gatewayrelays readings to the Energy Management System (EMS) via
Modbus/TCP or IEC 61850. This link is typically on-site LAN with sub-second latency.
4. Cloud telemetrystreams from the EMS to the dispatch controller (ORIUS) over
cellular, fibre, or satellite uplink. This link is the primary source of packet loss, delay
jitter, and reordering.
5. Grid-side meters(revenue meters, PMUs) provide independent power and frequency
measurements at 4s or sub-second resolution.
Each hop introduces a distinct failure mode: CAN bus saturation at high C-rates, SCADA
gateway reboot during firmware updates, cellular dropout during storms, and satellite latency
during congestion. The telemetry fault taxonomy used in CPSBench (Chapter 5.2) mirrors
these real failure modes.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 54 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS23
3.1.5 Why Measurement Quality Enters the Control Problem
Classical dispatch formulations assume that the controller observes the true statezt at each
step. In practice the controller observes a degraded version˜zt that may differ fromzt in
timing, magnitude, or completeness. The gap betweenzt and ˜zt has three consequences for
safety:
1.SOC estimation error.If the reported SOC is stale by∆tsteps, the true SOC has
drifted by up toPmax·∆t/Ecap in normalised units, wherePmax is the rated power and
Ecap the nameplate energy.
2. Forecast conditioning error.The most recent load and renewables readings con-
dition the next forecast window. Corrupted readings propagate into biased intervals,
undermining conformal coverage guarantees.
3. Constraint tightening mismatch.A robust optimiser that tightens constraints
based onassumedinterval width may under-tighten if the true observation quality is
worse than assumed, or over-tighten if quality is better, sacrificing economic value.
The central thesis of this dissertation is that measurement quality must beexplicitly
scored and fed backinto the uncertainty set at each control step. The observation quality
estimator (OQE, Chapter 4.1) and the reliability-adaptive conformal certificate (RAC-Cert)
implement this feedback loop.
3.1.6 Chapter Summary
This chapter established the ORIUS system as a five-stage closed decision loop—Forecast,
Optimize, Dispatch, Measure, Monitor—operating over grid-scale battery storage. The
Optimize–Dispatch boundary was identified as the highest-risk transition because actions are
irreversible, observation-dependent, and coupled through SOC dynamics. Telemetry traverses
multiple communication layers, each introducing distinct degradation modes. Because
measurement quality directly affects SOC estimation, forecast conditioning, and constraint
tightening, it must enter the control formulation as a first-class variable. The remainder of
Part II develops the mathematical machinery to accomplish this.
3.2 Data, Telemetry, and Dataset Scope
Every empirical claim in this dissertation rests on two locked dataset surfaces: the Germany
OPSD archive and the United States EIA-930 feed. This chapter documents the signals, their

provenance, the telemetry pathways through which they enter the ORIUS loop, and the fault
modes that make degraded observation an ordinary operating condition rather than an edge
case.
3.2.1 Germany and United States Dataset Surface
The ORIUS evaluation spans two geographically and regulatorily distinct power systems.
Germany (OPSD).The Open Power System Data platform aggregates hourly generation,
load, and day-ahead price series from ENTSO-E and the German TSOs. The locked dataset
covers 2018-10-07 to 2020-09-30 (17377 hourly rows), with four primary targets:load_mw,
wind_mw, solar_mw, andprice_eur_mwh. Weather covariates (temperature, irradiance, wind
speed) are sourced from the Open-Meteo archive API and joined on UTC timestamp. The full
feature matrix contains 94 engineered columns after calendar encoding and lag construction.
United States (EIA-930).The U.S. Energy Information Administration Form 930 pro-
vides hourly demand and net generation by source for each balancing authority. Three regions
are retained:

3.2.2 Forecast Targets and Operational Channels
Each dataset surface exposes three operational forecast targets:
load_mw Net system load (total demand minus behind-the-meter solar), measured at the
transmission level. This is the primary signal for arbitrage scheduling.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 56 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS25
wind_mw Aggregate onshore and offshore wind generation. Wind exhibits high variance and
heavy tails, particularly during storm fronts, making calibration challenging.
solar_mw Aggregate photovoltaic generation. Solar follows a deterministic diurnal envelope
modulated by cloud cover; residual uncertainty is sharply bimodal (clear vs. overcast).
A fourth target,price_eur_mwh (DE only), drives the economic objective in the dispatch
optimiser. For US regions, a simplified tariff schedule or locational marginal price proxy is
used.
All targets are sampled at hourly resolution and aligned to UTC. The forecast horizon is
H = 48h with a stride of 1h, yielding a rolling-origin evaluation with 168 test windows per
week.
3.2.3 Observed Telemetry Versus Truth Trajectory
A central design choice in ORIUS is the separation oftruth stateandobserved state. At every
control stept:
• Thetruth trajectory{zt}records what actually happened at the plant: the true SOC,
the true net load, the true renewable generation. This trajectory is never available to
the controller in real time; it is logged by the CPSBench harness for post-hoc evaluation.
• Theobserved trajectory {˜zt}is what the controller actually sees after telemetry
faults have been applied. It may contain dropped readings (NaN), delayed values,
sensor spikes, stale repetitions, or drifted baselines.
The gap∥zt−˜zt∥is the observation error. All safety metrics (TSVR, severity) are
computed on the truth trajectory, while the controller’s decisions are computed on the
observed trajectory. This dual-trajectory architecture is the foundation of the CPSBench
evaluation protocol (Chapter 5.2).
3.2.4 Missingness, Corruption, and Signal Degradation
Telemetry faults fall into six categories, each modelled by a corresponding CPSBench fault
injector:
These fault types are not mutually exclusive. Thedrift_combo scenario in CPSBench ap-
plies all six simultaneously at configurable severity, representing a worst-case but operationally
plausible field condition.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 57 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS26
Table 3.2: Telemetry fault taxonomy and operational causes.
Fault type Mechanism Field cause

3.2.5 Why Telemetry Faults Are Ordinary, Not Edge Cases
Field data from commercial BESS installations suggests that telemetry degradation is the
norm rather than the exception:
• Cellular uplinks in rural wind-farm sites experience 2–8% packet loss during normal
operation, rising to 15–25% during severe weather.
• SCADA gateway reboots occur 1–3 times per month, each causing a 30–90s telemetry
blackout.
• Sensor drift in current transducers accumulates at 0.1–0.5% of full-scale per year, enough
to bias SOC estimates by 2–5MWh on a 100MW/400MWh system within six months.
• Firmware updates on BMS controllers occasionally freeze individual cell-group readings
for minutes until the watchdog timer resets the reporting thread.
A safety framework that assumes clean telemetry is therefore unsafe by construction.
The ORIUS evaluation treats faulted telemetry as thedefaultoperating condition and clean
telemetry as a special case (thenominalscenario with zero fault injection).
3.2.6 Artifact Contracts and Profile Lock
Each dataset surface is frozen at a specific commit hash and validated by a reproducibility
contract:
1. Row-count invariant.The ingestion pipeline asserts that the row count matches the
locked value inreports/publication/dataset_cards.csv.
2. Column schema.The feature matrix schema (column names, dtypes, nullable flags)
is checked against a frozen JSON schema at pipeline entry.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 58 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS27
3. Target coverage.All three forecast targets must have 100% non-null coverage in the
locked period. Any gap causes the pipeline to abort with a traceable error.
4. Hash verification.The SHA-256 hash of the serialised Parquet file is compared to
the locked value before any model training or evaluation proceeds.
These contracts ensure that no drift in upstream data sources can silently invalidate the
empirical results.
3.2.7 Scope Boundary
The dataset scope of this dissertation is intentionally bounded:
• Included:hourly bulk-power system signals (load, wind, solar, price) for four regions
across two continents.
• Excluded:sub-hourly (5-min, 1-min) data, distribution-level signals, behind-the-meter
solar, demand response, and frequency regulation signals.
• Rationale:the hourly resolution matches the natural arbitrage cycle of 2–4h duration
storage and aligns with the temporal granularity of conformal calibration windows.
Extending the framework to sub-hourly resolution is discussed in Chapter 5.3 as part of
the benchmark roadmap.
3.2.8 Chapter Summary
This chapter documented the two dataset surfaces (Germany OPSD and US EIA-930) that
underpin every empirical result, the three forecast targets (load_mw, wind_mw, solar_mw), and
the six telemetry fault types that degrade observations between the plant and the controller.
The dual-trajectory architecture—truth state versus observed state—was introduced as
the foundation for safety evaluation. Artifact contracts and hash-locked profiles ensure
reproducibility. Telemetry faults are treated as ordinary operating conditions, not edge cases,
motivating the observation-aware machinery developed in the following chapters.
3.3 The Observed-State Safety Illusion
A battery controller that checks constraints only against its own observations will, by
construction, never detect a violation it cannot see. This chapter formalises theobserved-
state safety illusion, shows how it arises in the ORIUS dispatch loop, explains why neither

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 59 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS28
better forecasting nor tighter optimisation can resolve it, and introduces the dual-trajectory
evaluation paradigm that makes the illusion visible.
3.3.1 From Partial Observability to Safety Illusion
Partial observability is a well-studied concept in control theory and reinforcement learning.
A partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) acknowledges that the agent sees a
noisy or incomplete projection of the true state and plans accordingly, typically via belief-state
updates. The safety illusion arises when the agentdoes not plan accordingly—when the
controller treats its observation as ground truth and evaluates constraints against it without
accounting for observation quality.
In a POMDP with a well-calibrated belief, the agent maintains a distribution over true
states and can compute the worst-case constraint satisfaction probability. In practice, battery
dispatch controllers do not maintain belief distributions; they receive a point estimateˆxt
from the SCADA system and optimise against it. The gap between the POMDP ideal and
the deployed reality is the source of the illusion.
3.3.2 Why the Illusion Arises in the ORIUS Loop
The Optimise-to-Dispatch Boundary
As described in Chapter 1.2, the ORIUS loop has a clear boundary between the optimiser and
the dispatcher. The optimiser solves(1.3) on the observed state and produces a candidate
action a⋆
t. The dispatcher (in the baseline case) forwards this action to the plant without
modification.
Both the optimiser and the baseline dispatcher operate onˆxt. The safety constraints
SOCmin ≤[SOCt + ηc[at]+−[at]−/ηd
Emax
·∆t≤SOCmax
are checked on the observed SOC. If[SOCt over- or under-estimates the true SOC, the
constraint check is meaningless with respect to physical safety.
Why Good Forecasting Does Not Solve It
A natural objection is: “Improve the forecast, and the observation error disappears.” This
is incorrect for a fundamental reason. The forecast model predicts future load, solar, and
windgiven current observations. If the current observation is itself degraded—a stale SOC

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 60 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS29
reading, a dropped load packet—the forecast inherits the error. A perfect forecast model
with imperfect inputs produces imperfect outputs.
More precisely, the forecast model learnsE[Yt+h|ˆXt], not E[Yt+h|Xt]. When ˆXt̸=Xt,
the conditional expectation is evaluated at the wrong conditioning point. The resulting action
is optimal for a state the plant does not occupy.
3.3.3 Formal Statement of the Illusion
Definition 3.1(Observed-State Safety Illusion).Let ˆxt be the observed state,xt the true
state, and at the dispatched action. Theobserved-state safety illusionat time t is the
conjunction of three conditions:
1. Observed safety:at∈A(ˆxt)—the action satisfies all constraints when evaluated on
the observation.
2. True-state violation:at /∈A(xt)—the action violates at least one constraint when
evaluated on the truth.
3. Undetectability:The controller has no mechanism to detect condition (2) from its
available informationˆxt alone.
Condition (3) distinguishes the illusion from a detectable fault. If the controller could
infer that ˆxt is unreliable (e.g., via an OQE score), it could compensate. The illusion issilent:
the controller proceeds with full confidence in a physically unsafe action.
Theorem 3.2(Existence of the illusion under dropout).Under the fault model of Section 1.2.4
with dropout rateρ >0and last-value hold imputation, there exists a time stept and a
deterministic LP dispatch actionat such that the observed-state safety illusion (Definition 3.1)
holds with positive probability.
Proof sketch.Consider a discharge phase whereSOCtrue
t = SOCmin +ϵfor smallϵ>0. If the
most recentk SOC readings were dropped and the last-value hold reports[SOCt = SOCmin+ϵ+
k·δ(the SOC at the time of the last successful reading, beforek discharge steps each consuming
δ), then the LP observes a comfortable margin and dispatches a further dischargeat < 0.
The true SOC after this action isSOCtrue
t+1 = SOCmin +ϵ−|at|·∆t/(Emax·ηd) <SOC min
for|at|exceeding ϵ·Emax·ηd/∆t. The action satisfies observed constraints, violates true
constraints, and the LP cannot detect the violation—all three conditions hold.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 61 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS30
3.3.4 Battery-Domain Realisation Under Telemetry Faults
A Simple Battery Narrative
Consider a 100 MWh / 50 MW battery operating over 168 hours (one week) under the
dropout_onlyscenario atρ= 0.20.
1. Hour 0–24.Telemetry is clean. The deterministic LP dispatches charge during
solar surplus and discharge during evening peak. Observed and true SOC tracks are
indistinguishable.
2. Hour 25.A dropout burst begins: five consecutive SOC readings are missing. The
last-value hold freezes[SOCat 55%.
3. Hour 26–28.The LP continues discharging against the frozen observation. True SOC
falls from 55% to 38% to 24%. The observed SOC remains at 55%.
4. Hour 29.The LP dispatches a 40 MW discharge, believing SOC is 55%. True SOC
drops to 16%, breaching the 20% floor. Observed SOC still shows 55%. The illusion is
complete.
5. Hour 30.Telemetry recovers. The LP observes SOC at 16% and abruptly curtails
dispatch, but the damage—an SOC excursion below the safety floor—has already
occurred.
This narrative is not hypothetical. The CPSBench harness reproduces it deterministically
(seed 42,dropout_only, ρ= 0.20), and the true-state violation is recorded in the TSVR
metric.
3.3.5 Why Standard Evaluation Misses the Problem
Standard battery dispatch evaluation protocols report:
•Revenue or regret (economic performance).
•Observed SOC constraint violations (observed safety).
•Forecast accuracy (MAPE, RMSE, CRPS).
None of these metrics involve thetrueSOC trajectory. A controller that achieves zero
observed violations can simultaneously have a high true-state violation rate. The evaluation
is complicit in the illusion: it checks safety on the same degraded observation that caused
the unsafe action.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 62 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS31
Proposition 3.3(Insufficiency of observed-state evaluation).For anyTSVR> 0, there exists
a controller-scenario pair withOSVR = 0and the given TSVR. Observed-state evaluation is
thereforeinsufficientto certify physical safety.
Proof. Take any controllerπand any fault scenario with dropout rateρ >0. Since π
evaluates constraints onˆxt and the telemetry operator preserves observed-state feasibility by
construction (the hold value is always within bounds),OSVR = 0. By Theorem 3.2, there
exists a step whereat violates true-state constraints, soTSVR>0.
3.3.6 CPSBench and Dual-Trajectory Evaluation
To break the illusion, we introduce thedual-trajectoryevaluation paradigm implemented in
the CPSBench harness. At each stept, the harness maintains two parallel state machines:
1. Observed trajectory.The SOC evolution computed from the action at and the
observed stateˆxt, using the dynamics (1.1) with[SOCt as the initial condition.
2. True trajectory.The SOC evolution computed from thesameaction at but with the
true stateSOC true
t as the initial condition.
The true trajectory is never visible to the controller; it is maintained solely for evaluation.
This separation is critical: the controller cannot “cheat” by observing the truth, and the
evaluator cannot be fooled by the observation.
Definition 3.4(True-State Violation Rate (TSVR)).

The dual-trajectory architecture enables unambiguous attribution: every true-state viola-

CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS32
3.3.7 Why the Illusion Demands a Runtime Shield
The illusion cannot be resolved at the optimiser level for two reasons:
1. Information asymmetry.The optimiser has access only toˆxt. Without a reliability
signal, it cannot distinguish clean telemetry from degraded telemetry. Any fixed margin
it applies will be either too conservative (wasting revenue when telemetry is clean) or
too aggressive (missing violations when telemetry degrades).
2. Separation of concerns.The optimiser’s objective is economic; the safety layer’s
objective is physical. Mixing the two creates Pareto conflicts that are better resolved
by a dedicated post-optimiser shield. The shield can adapt its tightening in real time
based on the OQE score, something a batch optimiser solved minutes ago cannot do.
The DC3S shield resolves the illusion by introducing anobservation-quality-awarecon-
straint tightening that operates at the dispatch boundary. The shield does not replace the
optimiser; it corrects the optimiser’s blind spot.
Theorem 3.6(DC3S Feasibility Guarantee).Let α∈(0, 1)be the conformal miscoverage
level,n the calibration-set size, and¯w=T−1∑T
t=1wt the episode-mean reliability score. Under
Assumptions A1, A2, A3, and A5, the DC3S shield with conformal quantileˆqαinflated by
1/wt satisfies, for any finite episode ofTsteps:
TSVR≤α(1−¯w) +εn,
whereεn =O(1/√n)is the finite-sample calibration correction term. In particular:
1. At¯w= 1(perfect telemetry) and largen:TSVR→0.
2. At ¯w <1(degraded telemetry): the bound is proportional to the mean reliability gap
(1−¯w).
3. Asn→∞: εn→0and the bound converges to the episode-mean reliability term exactly.
This result is the single-episode analogue of Theorem T3 (ORIUS Core Bound, Chapter 4.5),
which provesE[V]≤α(1−¯w)Tin expectation over episodes.
The formal version of this theorem, with precise assumptions and a complete proof, is
developed in subsequent chapters.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 64 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS33
3.3.8 Chapter Summary
The observed-state safety illusion is the mechanism by which the OASG operates in practice:
a controller evaluates constraints on a degraded observation, concludes the system is safe,
and dispatches an action that is physically unsafe. The illusion is invisible to any evalua-
tion protocol that checks constraints on the observed trajectory only. The dual-trajectory
architecture of CPSBench makes the illusion visible, and the DC3S runtime shield makes it
preventable. The remainder of the dissertation develops the shield, proves its guarantees,
and validates them empirically across US and German grid data under the full fault model of
Chapter 1.2.
3.4 Battery Dynamics and Dispatch Problem
This chapter formalises the state-space model of a grid-scale battery energy storage system
(BESS), derives the dispatch optimisation programme, and shows how observation uncertainty
enlarges the feasible set in ways that classical deterministic formulations cannot capture.
3.4.1 Battery State Variables
The BESS plant is characterised by the following state and parameter variables at each hourly
time stept:

Pmax = 200MW, η+ =η−= 0.95, andRmax =Pmax (no additional ramp constraint beyond
rated power). Soft SOC margins are enforced at[SOCmin,SOC max] = [0.05, 0.95]; hard BMS

3.4.6 Uncertainty and Feasible Set Under Degraded Observation
When the observation˜Et is degraded, the initial-condition constraintEt = ˜Et in (3.8) becomes

For the SOC bounds to hold with probability at least1−α, the constraint must be tightened

where qt is the reliability-inflated interval half-width from RAC-Cert (Chapter 4.1). This
tightening shrinks the feasible set and reduces the economic value of the dispatch, but
guarantees safety against observation error.
3.4.7 Calibration Gap and Uncertainty Mismatch
Even with conformal intervals, acalibration gaparises when marginal coverage differs from
conditional coverage across reliability groups. The locked CQR group-coverage results
(reports/publication/cqr_group_coverage.csv) reveal that wind intervals achieve only
39–49% coverage in the low and high reliability groups despite 90% nominal marginal
coverage. Solar intervals are better calibrated (82–90%) but still exhibit under-coverage at
the high-variability group.
This calibration gap means that the tightened bounds in(3.10) areinsufficiently tight
during periods of poor observation quality, motivating the adaptive inflation law in DC3S
(Section 3.4.5 cross-references Chapter 4.1).
3.4.8 Baseline Policies
The ORIUS evaluation includes eight dispatch controllers spanning four design philosophies:
1. Deterministic LP( deterministic_lp): solves (3.8) on point forecasts with no
interval awareness.
2. Robust fixed-interval(robust_fixed_interval): worst-case robust LP using fixed
(non-adaptive) prediction intervals.
3. CVaR interval(cvar_interval): CVaR-optimised scenario dispatch over quantile
forecasts.
4. ACI conformal(aci_conformal): adaptive conformal inference baseline with online
width adjustment.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 68 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS37
5. Scenariorobust( scenario_robust): scenario-basedrobustoptimisationoversampled
forecast paths.
6. Scenario MPC( scenario_mpc): receding-horizon model predictive control with
scenario tree.
7.DC3S wrapped(dc3s_wrapped): full DC3S shield with linear reliability inflation.
8.DC3S FTIT(dc3s_ftit): DC3S with fault-tolerant invariant tube extension.
Results across these controllers are reported in Chapter 4.1 and the benchmark track
(Chapter 5.2).
3.4.9 Scope Boundary
The dispatch model in this dissertation is asingle-asset, energy-onlyformulation:
• Included:energy arbitrage, SOC management, degradation penalty, safety constraints.
• Excluded:ancillary services (frequency regulation, spinning reserve), multi-asset
portfolio dispatch, network constraints, and market-clearing co-optimisation.
The single-asset scope is sufficient to demonstrate the observation-quality feedback mech-
anism; extension to fleet-level dispatch is discussed in Chapter 5.3.
3.4.10 Chapter Summary
This chapter formalised the battery state-space model, the SOC dynamics equation(3.1),
the power/ramp/SOC constraint set, and the deterministic dispatch LP(3.8). Three action
types—candidate, safe, and executed—were distinguished to precisely locate the safety
intervention point. The effect of degraded observation on the feasible set was derived, showing
that the conformal half-width must tighten the SOC bounds and that calibration gaps in
wind and solar intervals motivate adaptive inflation. Eight baseline controllers spanning
deterministic, robust, conformal, and shielded design philosophies were enumerated.

Chapter 4
ORIUS Framework and Theoretical
Foundations
4.1 DC3S: The Universal Safety Adapter
The Detect–Calibrate–Constrain–Shield–Certify Safety System (DC3S) is the runtime adapter
that sits at the Optimize–Dispatch boundary (Chapter 3.1) and translates degraded obser-
vations into safety-guaranteed actions. This chapter specifies the runtime objects, derives
each stage of the pipeline, and narrates a complete control step from telemetry ingestion to
certificate issuance.
4.1.1 Why a Runtime Shield Is Needed
Chapter 5.1 showed that CQR intervals under-cover conditionally: wind coverage drops to
39–49% in the low-reliability group, and load coverage falls 8–20 percentage points below
nominal. A dispatch controller that trusts these intervals without adjustment will issue
actions that violate SOC constraints on the true (unobserved) trajectory at rates far exceeding
the design budgetα.
A runtime shield is needed because:
1. Forecast calibration is abatchproperty; telemetry degradation is astreamingproperty.
No static calibration fold can anticipate the specific fault pattern at stept.
2. The observation quality at stept isknownto the controller (via packet-loss counters,
staleness timers, and drift detectors) but isnot usedby standard conformal methods.
3. The SOC dynamics arecoupled: an unsafe action att may render all future actions
infeasible. The shield must prevent the first violation, not merely reduce its frequency.
38

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 70 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS39

4.1.3 Detect: Telemetry Reliability Scoring (OQE)
The Observation Quality Estimator (OQE) computes the reliability scorewt∈[0, 1]from
three sub-scores:
wt =w fresh
t ·wrange
t ·wconsist
t ,(4.1)
where:
wfresh
t Freshness score:exp(−λf·st), decaying exponentially with the stale countst.
wrange
t Range plausibility: 1 if all channels are within physical bounds, decayed proportionally
to the number of out-of-range channels.
wconsist
t Temporal consistency: based on the Mahalanobis distance of the current reading
from a short rolling window, penalising spikes and discontinuities.
The OQE computation is stateless given the rolling buffer and runs in< 0.03ms median
latency (Table 4.3,reports/publication/dc3s_latency_summary.csv).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 71 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS40
4.1.4 Calibrate: Reliability-Adaptive Interval Inflation
The base CQR intervalCt(α)is inflated to compensate for the conditional under-coverage
revealed in Chapter 5.1. TheRAC-Cert(Reliability-Adaptive Conformal Certificate) formula
is:
CRAC
t (α) =Ct(α)·
[
1 +κr (1−wt) +κddt +κsst
]
(4.2)
where:
κr Reliability sensitivity: scales the inflation with the complement of the reliability score.
Defaultκr = 0.5.
κd Drift sensitivity: additive inflation when the Page–Hinkley detector fires. Defaultκd = 0.3.
κs Staleness sensitivity: linear inflation per stale step. Defaultκs = 0.05.
The multiplicative form ensures that under nominal conditions (wt = 1, dt = 0, st = 0)
the inflated interval collapses to the base interval, imposing zero additional conservatism.
Inflationdiagnostics.Thelockedevidencein reports/publication/dc3s_main_table.csv
reports the mean and P95 of the RAC inflation factor across all scenarios and seeds. Under
the drift_combo scenario the mean inflation is approximately1.02, rising to1.05at the P95
level, confirming that the adaptive layer adds modest width only when observation quality
genuinely degrades.

4.1.7 FTIT State Tube
The Fault-Tolerant Invariant Tube (FTIT) provides a secondary safety layer that does not
depend on interval calibration. FTIT propagates worst-case SOC bounds forward over a
k-step horizon:
Et+k = ˆEt +δmax +kη+P +
max ∆t,
Et+k = ˆEt−δmax−kP−
max/η−∆t,
(4.5)
where δmax is the maximum SOC estimation error consistent with the current reliability
score. Any action that would cause either bound to exit[Emin,E max]within the k-step tube
is rejected.
The FTIT acts as a hard backstop: even if the RAC-Cert inflation is insufficient (e.g.
due to a novel fault type not captured by the sensitivity parameters), the tube prevents
multi-step SOC excursions.

5.Shield.Ifa ⋆

6. Certify.Emit the certificateCt recording all runtime variables, the intervention decision,
and the guarantee-check outcome. Forwardasafe
t to the BMS.
4.1.11 Chapter Summary
This chapter specified the DC3S battery adapter: a five-stage runtime shield (Detect, Cali-
brate, Constrain, Shield, Certify) that intercepts dispatch actions at the Optimize–Dispatch
boundary. The OQE reliability scorer(4.1) quantifies observation quality; the RAC-Cert
formula (4.2) inflates intervals adaptively; the safe-action set(4.3) tightens SOC bounds
accordingly; and the projection repair(4.4) moves unsafe actions to the nearest feasible point.
The FTIT tube provides a calibration-free safety backstop. The full pipeline completes in
under 0.2ms at P99, and step-level certificates create a complete audit trail. Empirical
validation is presented in Chapter 5.2.
4.2 Assumptions, Notation, and Proof Discipline
This chapter establishes the formal scaffolding for Part III. It fixes notation that is stable
across all theorems, catalogues the eight standing assumptions, defines the proof dependency
matrix, and sets the disciplinary standards that every subsequent proof must satisfy. The
flagship DC3S ladder in Chapters 4.3–4.7 contains eight principal theorems (T1–T8), but
it is not the entire theorem surface of the dissertation. Part I establishes two foundational
precursor theorems (S1: illusion existence under dropout; S2: informal DC3S guarantee);

assumptions ground every formal claim. The integrated surface totals67 formal items
(18 theorems, 5 lemmas, 7 propositions, 10 corollaries, 11 definitions, 16 assumptions), all
audited in Appendix ac and summarised in Table 4.5 at the end of this chapter.

4.2.2 Assumption IDs (A1–A8)
Each assumption is assigned a permanent identifier and stated in both domain-general and
battery-specific form.
Assumption 1(A1: Bounded model error).The one-step prediction error∥xt+1−f(xt,at)∥
is bounded by a known constantϵmodel.Battery:|xt+1−(xt +ηPt ∆t)|≤ϵmodel.
Assumption 2(A2: Bounded telemetry error).The observation error∥ˆxt−xt∥is bounded
by a reliability-dependent function:∥ˆxt−xt∥≤g(wt), wheregis monotone decreasing.
Assumption 3(A3: Feasible safe repair).For every state xt satisfying ϕ(xt) = 1, there
exists at least one actionat∈At such thatϕ(xt+1) = 1.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 76 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS45
Assumption 4(A4: Known dynamics).The transition functionf(xt,at)is known to the
controller up to the model error bounded in A1.Battery:the SOC transition xt+1 =
xt +ηPt ∆tis known with efficiencyηidentified offline.
Assumption 5(A5: Monotone bounded uncertainty inflation).The inflation function
ι(wt) =q t/(wt +ϵ)is monotone decreasing inwt and bounded:ι(wt)≤¯ιfor allwt∈[0,1].
Assumption 6(A6: Bounded detector lag).The OQE reliability scorewt reflects a telemetry
fault within at mostℓsteps of fault onset: if a fault begins at steptf, thenwtf +ℓ≤walert.
Assumption 7(A7: Causal certificate update).The certificateCt depends only on informa-
tion available at or before stept:Ct =h(ˆx1:t,a 1:t−1).
Assumption 8(A8: Admissible fallback policy).There exists a fallback policyπfb that,
starting from any safe state, maintainsϕ(xt) = 1for all future steps under worst-case bounded
disturbance.Battery:the zero-dispatch policy ( Pt = 0) is always admissible because SOC is
unchanged (up to model error).

4.2.4 Marginal Versus Conditional Coverage
A critical distinction throughout Part III:
Marginal coverage.Pr [xt∈ˆCt ]≥1−αaveraged over the randomness of calibration and
test points. This is what CQR provides.
Conditional coverage.Pr [xt∈ˆCt|ˆxt ]≥1−αfor every observed value. Conditional cov-
erage is strictly stronger and isnotguaranteed by CQR without additional distributional
assumptions.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 77 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS46
The ORIUS theorems are stated under marginal coverage. Where conditional coverage
is needed (e.g., for the per-step risk lemma), we explicitly invoke the monotone inflation
assumption (A5) as a surrogate.
4.2.5 One-Step Versus Multi-Step Claims
The theorems in Chapters 4.3–4.5 operate on aper-stepbasis: each step’s safety is analysed
conditionally on the current state. The core bound (Chapter 4.5) aggregates per-step risks
into an episode-level expectation via linearity of expectation.
Multi-step claims (certificate horizon, graceful degradation) require additional assumptions
about temporal dependence; these are stated explicitly in Chapter 4.7.
4.2.6 The Theorem Ladder
The theorems form a logical ladder:
1. OASG Existence(Chapter 4.3): The problem is real—observed safety does not imply
true safety.
2. Safety Preservation(Chapter 4.4): The shield solves the problem—true safety is
restored.
3. Core Bound(Chapter 4.5): The solution has a quantifiable cost—expected violations
are bounded.
4. No Free Safety(Chapter 4.6): The cost is unavoidable—ignoring observation quality
breaks safety.
5. Temporal Extensions(Chapter 4.7): The guarantee extends across time—certificates
expire gracefully.
Each rung depends on the ones below it, and no rung can be removed without breaking
the chain.
4.2.7 Theorem-Writing Discipline
Every theorem in this dissertation follows a fixed template:
1.Statement: formal claim with quantifiers.
2.Assumptions used: list of A-IDs.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 78 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS47
3.Proof: complete derivation.
4. Artifact link: pointer to the empirical evidence that instantiates the theorem in the
battery domain.
5.Scope disclaimer: “What this theorem does not prove.”
4.2.8 Common Theorem Failure Modes
To inoculate against reviewer objections, we catalogue failure modes that a careless proof
might exhibit:
1. Vacuous truth.If the assumption set is never satisfied, the theorem is true but useless.
We verify non-vacuousness by exhibiting the battery instantiation.
2. Circular dependence.Theorem X assumes the conclusion of TheoremY, and vice versa.
The dependency matrix (Table 4.4) prevents this.
3. Hidden conditional.A marginal claim is stated as if it were conditional. We use the
marginal/conditional distinction explicitly.
4. Infinite-sample limit.A result that holds only asymptotically is presented as finite-
sample. All ORIUS bounds are stated for finiteT.
4.2.9 Integrated Theorem Surface Summary
Table 4.5 enumerates the full formal surface of the dissertation. The ten unique theorems (S1,
S2, T1–T8) and their appendix restatements (C.1–C.9) all pass the integrated theorem gate:
source file exists, code anchor verified, artifact locked, and appendix mapping confirmed.
4.2.10 Chapter Summary
This chapter provides the stable notational, assumptive, and methodological foundation for
every proof in Part III. The eight assumptions (A1–A8) are individually justified in both
domain-general and battery-specific terms, the proof dependency matrix prevents circular
reasoning, and the theorem-writing discipline ensures that every claim is bounded by explicit
scope disclaimers. The integrated theorem surface of 67 formal items—spanning 18 theorems,
5 lemmas, 7 propositions, 10 corollaries, 11 definitions, and 16 assumptions—is the formal
depth this dissertation contributes to the CPS safety literature.

4.3 Theorem 1: OASG Existence
This chapter proves theObserved-safe / Actually-unsafe State Gap(OASG) existence theorem
in the battery domain. The theorem establishes that telemetry degradation creates episodes
where an action is safe according to the observed state but unsafe according to the true
state—the foundational phenomenon that motivates the entire ORIUS safety layer.
4.3.1 Domain-Agnostic Statement
We first state the OASG existence result for an arbitrary cyber-physical system; the battery
domain provides one concrete instantiation below (Chapter 7.1 covers all six).
Definition 4.1(Abstract CPS).A cyber-physical system is a tuple(X,U,f,S,ˆx )where
X ⊆Rn is the state space,U⊆Rm is the action space,f :X×U→Xis the (possibly
stochastic) dynamics,S⊆Xis the safe set with non-empty boundary∂S̸=∅, andˆxt =xt+ξt
is the observed state under telemetry errorξt with∥ξt∥≤g(wt)(Assumption A2).
Theorem 4.2(OASG Existence — General CPS).Let(X,U,f,S,ˆx )be a CPS satisfying
Assumptions A1 (bounded dynamics error), A2 (bounded telemetry error withg(wt) > 0
under degraded quality), and A4 (known dynamics). Letπ:X→Ube any quality-ignorant
controller, i.e. one whose action depends only onˆxt and not onwt.
If the controller drives the state within distanceδ >0of ∂Swith positive frequency, and
the telemetry error satisfiesPr[∥ξt∥>δ]>0, then there exists a timet∗such that
ϕobs(π(ˆxt∗)) = 1andϕ true(π(ˆxt∗)) = 0,
whereϕobs andϕtrue are the observed-safe and truly-safe predicates respectively.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 80 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS49
The proof constructs the witnesst∗by intersecting the boundary-proximity set (from
optimal control) with the observation-error tail (from degraded telemetry). We now instantiate
this in the battery domain.
4.3.2 Battery-Domain Instantiation
Consider a battery energy storage system with:
•True statex t =SOC t∈[0,Emax](MWh).
• Observed state ˆxt =xt +ξt, whereξt is the telemetry error induced by packet dropout,
delay, drift, or stale sensors.
•Actiona t = (Pchg
t ,P dis
t )∈R2

• Dynamics: xt+1 =xt + (ηcPchg
t −η−1
d Pdis
t ) ∆t +ϵt, where|ϵt|≤ϵmodel (Assumption A1).
4.3.3 Observed and True Safety Predicates
Definition 4.3(Observed safety).An action at isobserved-safeif the predicted next state
under the observed SOC satisfies the constraints:
ϕobs(at) =1
[

ϕtrue(at) =1

]

The OASG phenomenon occurs whenϕobs(at) = 1butϕtrue(at) = 0.
4.3.4 Assumptions Used
The OASG existence theorem requires:
•A1(bounded model error): ensures the dynamics are predictable up toϵmodel.
• A2(bounded telemetry error): ensures the observation gap|ξt|≤g(wt)is finite but
non-zero under degraded telemetry.
•A4(known dynamics): ensures the controller can compute∆(a t)correctly.

4.3.5 Auxiliary Lemmas
Lemma 4.5(Observation gap under dropout).Under packet dropout with rated> 0, the
observation error satisfies
E
[
|ξt|
]
≥d·σdrift·
√
∆t,
whereσdrift is the standard deviation of the one-step state drift.
Proof. When a packet is dropped (probabilityd), the observer uses the last received state,
which drifts by at leastσdrift
√
∆t in expectation under the sub-Gaussian drift model. The
result follows by the law of total expectation.
Lemma 4.6(Boundary proximity under arbitrage).An optimal arbitrage controller drives
SOC withinδbnd =Pmax ∆t of at least one constraint bound in at least a fractionfbnd > 0of
time steps.
Proof. Optimal arbitrage charges to near-full when prices are low and discharges to near-
empty when prices are high. The fractionfbnd depends on the price distribution and is
strictly positive whenever price variance is non-zero.
4.3.6 Main Theorem: OASG Existence
Theorem 4.7(OASG Existence).Under Assumptions A1, A2, and A4, for any dropout rate
d>0and any optimal arbitrage controller, there exists a time stept∗such that
ϕobs(at∗) = 1andϕ true(at∗) = 0.
That is, the controller selects an action that appears safe in the observed state but violates a
constraint in the true state.
Proof. By Lemma 4.6, there exists a setTbnd⊂{1,...,T}with|Tbnd|≥fbndT such that for
t∈Tbnd, the true SOC is withinδbnd of a constraint bound.
By Lemma 4.5, for dropout rated> 0the observation error has E[|ξt|]> 0. In particular,
with positive probability,|ξt|>δbnd for somet∈Tbnd.
At such a stept∗, the observed stateˆxt∗ =xt∗ +ξt∗ is displaced from the true state by
more than the distance to the constraint boundary. The controller, which sees onlyˆxt∗,
selects an action that is feasible under the observed state but pushes the true state across
the bound:
xt∗ + ∆(at∗)<SOC min whileˆx t∗ + ∆(at∗)≥SOCmin.
Henceϕobs(at∗) = 1andϕtrue(at∗) = 0.

4.3.9 Corollaries
Corollary 4.8(OASG rate lower bound).Under the conditions of Theorem 4.7, the expected
OASG rate satisfiesE[TSVR]≥d·fbnd·Pr[|ξt|>δbnd]>0.
Corollary 4.9(OASG severity).The expected severity of an OASG violation is at least
E[|ξt|−δbnd||ξt|>δbnd]>0.
4.3.10 What This Theorem Does Not Prove
1. It does not quantify theexactTSVR for a specific controller; that requires empirical
measurement (Chapter 6.1).
2. It does not claim thateverystep has a violation, only that at least one exists.
3. It does not address whether the violation can be prevented—that is the subject of
Theorem 4.14.
4.3.11 Chapter Summary
The OASG Existence theorem is the first rung of the theorem ladder. It proves that the gap
between observed and true safety is not a theoretical curiosity but an inevitable consequence
of telemetry degradation in optimising controllers. The energy management domain provides
a concrete witness: a TSVR of 3.9% for the deterministic LP underdrift_combo at 20%
dropout.

4.4.2 Battery-Domain Instantiation: Tightened Safe Action Set
Definition 4.11(Tightened safe-action set).Given observed state ˆxt, reliability scorewt,
and conformal quantileqt, the tightened safe-action set is
At =
{
a∈U:SOC min +mt ≤ˆxt + ∆(a)≤SOC max−mt
}
,
where the marginmt =qt/(wt +ϵ)is the reliability-inflated conformal half-width andU is
the power/ramp feasible set.
The marginmt grows as reliabilitywt decreases, shrinkingAt and forcing the controller
toward more conservative actions.
4.4.3 Assumptions Used
The Safety Preservation theorem requires:
•A1(bounded model error): one-step prediction error|ϵt|≤ϵmodel.
•A2(bounded telemetry error):|ˆxt−xt|≤g(wt).
•A3(feasible safe repair):A t̸=∅wheneverϕ(xt) = 1.
•A4(known dynamics): the controller can compute∆(a).
•A5(monotone bounded inflation):m t is monotone inwt and bounded above.
•A7(causal certificate): no future information is used.
4.4.4 Auxiliary Propositions
Proposition 4.12(Margin dominates observation gap).Under Assumptions A2 and A5,
the margin satisfiesmt≥|ˆxt−xt|with probability at least1−α.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 86 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS55
Proof. By the conformal guarantee,xt∈[ˆxt−qt,ˆxt +qt]with probability ≥1−α. Since
mt =qt/(wt +ϵ)≥qt whenwt +ϵ≤1(which holds for degraded telemetry), and the inflated
interval[ ˆxt−mt,ˆxt +mt]contains the conformal interval, we have|xt−ˆxt|≤mt with
probability≥1−α.
Proposition 4.13(Tightened feasibility implies true feasibility).Ifat∈At and|xt−ˆxt|≤mt,
thenϕtrue(at) = 1.
Proof.By Definition 4.11,ˆxt + ∆(at)∈[SOCmin +mt,SOC max−mt]. Since|xt−ˆxt|≤mt,
xt + ∆(at)∈[ˆxt−mt + ∆(at),ˆxt +mt + ∆(at)]⊆[SOCmin,SOC max].
Adding the model error|ϵt| ≤ϵmodel and noting that the margin implicitly includes a
model-error buffer completes the bound.
4.4.5 Main Theorem: Safety Preservation
Theorem 4.14(Safety Preservation).Under Assumptions A1–A5 and A7, if the DC3S
shield selectsat∈At at every step, then
Pr
[
ϕtrue(at) = 1
]
≥1−αfor allt= 1,...,T.
In particular, the expected number of true-state violations satisfiesE[V]≤αT.
Proof. Fix an arbitrary stept. The DC3S shield selects at∈At. By Proposition 4.12,
|xt−ˆxt|≤mt with probability≥1−α. Conditioned on this event, Proposition 4.13 gives
ϕtrue(at) = 1. HencePr[ϕtrue(at) = 1]≥1−α.
Summing over all steps and applying linearity of expectation:E[V ] =∑T
t=1 Pr[ϕtrue(at) =
0]≤αT.
